All finite-length homotopy groups are finite by Lemma 9.5; their inverse systems are Mittag–Leffler,
so the Milnor lim1 -term vanishes in every stem. The compatible generators zℓ therefore give an
actual class δ, and their inverse limit is Zp . The last assertion follows from the equality of the
constant cochain maps in Lemma 9.4 and from the natural edge identification in Lemma 9.5.

Remark 9.8. The preceding argument does not identify the usual full Witt tower on the uncompleted
B0 with the ordinary Dℓ tower. For later use, the exact statement needed for any class from integral
P3 -constants is simpler. Its image in Wℓ (B0 ) is fixed by coefficient Frobenius, so (9.3) gives an
ordinary reduction-compatible family in Dℓ . The two cohomology isomorphisms in Lemma 9.3
preserve the order of each finite-length element. Hence unbounded p-power order of the corresponding
analytic Witt images implies unbounded order of these specified ordinary images. Combined with
Proposition 9.7, this is the bridge used by the rational detector in Section 10.

9.3   The actual height-one equivalence
Theorem 9.9. The integral classes ζ and δ above and the canonical unit define an equivalence
                                                                       ≃
                      (1, ζ, δ, ζδ) : R1 ∨ Σ−1 R1 ∨ Σ−3 R1 ∨ Σ−4 R1 −−→ LK(1) T.

The maps use the R1 -action and the multiplication induced from T . In particular the first two
components are the K(1)-localizations of the same maps from S used in Theorem 7.5.

Proof. The target is a K(1)-local algebra and hence a module over the local unit R1 . Each homotopy
class displayed defines an actual map from the indicated suspension of R1 . The product ζδ is formed
using this algebra multiplication, so no independent choice of a degree-minus-four line is being made.
    Modulo p, the source groups are the direct sum of the four shifts of

                                     π∗ (R1 /p) = Fp [v1±1 ] ⊗ Λ(h).

By Lemma 9.2, the images of 1, ζ, d, ζd, together with their h-multiples, are exactly the eight exterior
basis elements in the target coefficient calculation. The class δ reduces to d by Proposition 9.7.
Products are detected by the cup action, so these statements specify the leading filtered class
of each of the eight actual homotopy images. The spectral sequence has collapsed with finite
filtration. In each stem, filter the source homotopy group by assigning these eight periodic basis
elements the cohomological degrees of their displayed leading classes. The map is filtered, and
its associated-graded map is an isomorphism in every degree. Induction on the finite filtration,
using the short exact sequences for successive filtration quotients, shows that the original map of
homotopy groups is an isomorphism. This argument does not require an a priori splitting of the
target filtration. The displayed map is therefore an isomorphism on mod-p homotopy in every stem.
     Its cofiber is K(1)-local, hence derived p-complete by Lemma 9.6. The vanishing of its mod-p
quotient makes multiplication by p an equivalence, so all its quotients by pℓ vanish. Derived
completeness then makes the cofiber zero. This proves the equivalence, including the asserted
compatibility of its first two components.



                                                   83
10      The rational primitive and its localization
The integral basis gives a distinguished line Zp δ ⊂ π−3 LK(1) T . We now construct a rational class in
π−3 L0 T whose image is nonzero in its rational span Qp δ.
                                     ♭
    Fix C = Q p , with tilted base C and untilt divisor ∞. Let M classify P3 -structured forms E of
             c
V3 equipped with a quotient line at ∞. The lower modification E − is a slope-zero rank-three bundle,
hence a rational local system; its determinant inherits an integral lattice from the P3 -structure. The
two frame descriptions give

                                  M ≃ [P2C /P3 ] ≃ [Ω2C /J3 ],
                                   J3 = {g ∈ GL3 (Qp ) : vp (det g) = 0},

where Ω2C is the projective plane minus its Qp -rational hyperplanes. The first presentation records
quotient lines of a framed E; the second records upper modifications of a rationally framed E − with
the prescribed determinant lattice. These identifications, including their common arithmetic action,
are proved in Lemma 10.3.
    The projective presentation singles out the reduced-trace line from P3 as the arithmetic-invariant
part of rational degree-three cohomology. The Drinfeld presentation places a nonzero matrix-trace
class in that same line. Pulling their proportionality back along the ordinary sequence then restricts
the rank-three matrix trace to the trace of its original integral rank-two kernel. We retain these
actual classifying maps through finite Witt coefficients and exact Morava descent to identify the
nonzero image in Qp δ under localization.

The coefficients here are finite discrete modules with trivial action, and the cochains are the
inhomogeneous cochains of Section 3. At each finite level the cochain complex is the external derived
cohomology of the condensed classifying topos Bcond G, the topos of condensed sets with G-action.
Indeed, the standard resolution there has terms indexed by Ga . Finite discrete coefficients are solid,
and their higher condensed cohomology on a locally profinite set vanishes. Its degree-zero sections
are Ccts (Ga , Λℓ ), so the standard resolution gives the natural identification in the derived category

                                   RΓCond (Bcond G, Λℓ ) ≃ Ccts (G, Λℓ )

by [1, Section 2, standard resolution and Lemmas 2.1–2.2, pp. 5–7]. This is an external complex of
ordinary modules. We reserve RΓ(G, K) for internal condensed derived invariants of a condensed
coefficient object K with its actual G-action; evaluation at the point, denoted (∗), gives its external
complex.
    Twists are made at finite level. For compact G, reduction of finite cochains is surjective: lift

    We now specify how a group class enters relative geometric cohomology. For a small v-base
B, let GB = B × G be the constant locally profinite v-group and put BB G = [B/GB ], with trivial
action on B. Products with group parameters below are over this base.
Lemma 10.1 (Finite classifying maps). For a G-space X over B there is a natural finite coefficient
map
                      •
           uX,G,ℓ : Ccts (G, Λℓ ) −→ Tot[a]∈∆ RΓv (X × Ga , Λℓ ) ≃ RΓv ([X/G], Λℓ ).        (10.1)
For a G-torsor T → Z over B with relative classifying map cT : Z → BB G, this gives

                             clcT ,ℓ := c∗T uB,G,ℓ : Ccts
                                                      •
                                                          (G, Λℓ ) −→ RΓv (Z, Λℓ ).

It equals (10.1) for X = T under [T /G] ≃ Z. These maps commute with continuous group homo-
morphisms and extension of torsor structure, equivariant maps, base change, base automorphisms
with compatible descent, coefficient reductions, and cup products. They also commute with the finite
coefficient units Λℓ = Wℓ (Fp ) → Wℓ O♭ . Their derived coefficient limits and their rationalizations
define maps denoted clcT ,int and clcT ,b on the corresponding cohomology.
    For an algebraically closed perfectoid field C of characteristic zero, put BC = Spa(C, OC )⋄ . For
every profinite Q and every finite constant coefficient module A on BC , the actual constants map is
an equivalence
                                                 ≃
                                 Ccts (Q, A)[0] −−→ RΓv (BC × Q, A).                            (10.2)
This is natural in Q, in A, and in base automorphisms with their actions on A. In particular it
includes the finite Tate fibers Λℓ (−j). For compact G, it makes uBC ,G,ℓ an equivalence.
Proof. For a locally profinite parameter Q, regard AQ = Ccts (Q, Λℓ ) as a discrete ring. Evaluation
gives a map of ring sheaves on X × Q,

                                      AQ −→ Λℓ ,        (f, q) 7−→ f (q).

It is defined on the finitely many clopen fibers of each f ; this uses no compactness of Q. The unit
of the derived constant-sheaf/global-sections adjunction, followed by derived sections of evaluation,
gives
                                 Ccts (Q, Λℓ )[0] −→ RΓv (X × Q, Λℓ ).
Equivalently, this sends a continuous Λℓ -valued function on Q to its ordinary section and then to
derived sections. With Q = Ga , the projections of the action-groupoid nerve commute with all bar
faces and codegeneracies, including the action face. These maps therefore form a cosimplicial map.
Its totalization is (10.1) by quotient Čech descent.
    The map of nerves that forgets T over B proves the stated equality with c∗T uB,G,ℓ . For a
continuous homomorphism h : H → G, let S → Z be an H-torsor over B with a specified
identification S ×H G ≃ T over Z. The resulting map of torsor nerves acts in degree a by ha on
the transition elements. Evaluation commutes with these maps, with equivariant maps of X, and
with every base change. A base automorphism whose descent commutes with the constant group
acts on a torsor nerve by (t, g1 , . . . , ga ) 7→ (γ(t), g1 , . . . , ga ), which proves the asserted arithmetic
naturality. The construction uses maps of coefficient ring sheaves and the lax monoidal derived
sections functor, so it preserves cup products. Composing the same ring-sheaf maps with Λℓ → Λℓ−1
or with Λℓ = Wℓ (Fp ) → Wℓ O♭ proves the coefficient naturalities term by term. In particular these
are maps of the derived coefficient towers before cohomology is taken.

                                                      85
    For (10.2), use all finite clopen quotients Qi of Q. The example after Definition 7.8 and
Proposition 7.16 of [43] identify BC × Q with the inverse limit of BC × Qi and make BC a strictly
totally disconnected geometric point. Its étale covers split, so its finite constant cohomology is A[0].
Continuity and comparison with v-cohomology in [43, Propositions 14.9–14.10] now give

                       RΓv (BC × Q, A) ≃ colim Map(Qi , A)[0] = Ccts (Q, A)[0].
                                                 i

Every map here is induced by the coefficient unit and is natural in the finite partitions, coefficient
fiber, and base. This proves the stated naturality, including the arithmetic action on a Tate fiber.
Apply the equivalence to Q = Ga for compact G and totalize to obtain the last assertion. Over the
finite-field base used below we use the finite classifying map itself; this point comparison is only
asserted over BC .

where Q is profinite; these are derived sections on Q.

10.2    Trace classes and a parabolic subgroup
The invariant form
                                        (X, Y, Z) 7−→ Tr(X[Y, Z])
uses reduced trace for the division algebra and matrix trace for the split groups. This is the
classical degree-three cocycle attached to an invariant symmetric form, as in Chevalley–Eilenberg
[15, Section 21] and Koszul [31, Section 11]. The next lemma constructs the corresponding group-
cohomology classes and identifies the restriction needed for the ordinary kernel.
Lemma 10.2. The groups P3 , J3 , and GL2 (Zp ) have rational cohomology Hb1 = Qp , Hb2 = 0, and
Hb3 = Qp . They admit nonzero classes e3 , eJ , and e2 , respectively, whose rational Lie-algebra images
are nonzero multiples of the displayed trace form. For every compact open K ⊂ J3 , restriction
followed by the compact Lie-algebra comparison identifies Hb∗ (J3 ) with H ∗ (gl3 , Qp ); the identifications
for different K agree under further restriction. For
                                (         !                                          )
                                    A v
                          D=                  : A ∈ GL2 (Zp ),   a ∈ Z×
                                                                      p,   v ∈ Q2p       ,
                                    0 a

write ι : D → J3 and ρ : D → GL2 (Zp ) for inclusion and projection. Then

                                    ι∗ eJ = bρ∗ e2    for some b ∈ Q×
                                                                    p.                                (10.3)

Proof. We first make the continuous comparison on compact groups precise. Each compact group
K under consideration has an open normal uniform pro-p subgroup U . For P3 and GL2 (Zp ) one
may use a sufficiently deep principal congruence subgroup. A compact open K ⊂ J3 preserves a
lattice in Q3p : the orbit of any lattice is finite, and its sum is stable. A sufficiently deep principal
congruence subgroup for this lattice lies in K and is normal there. If the depth is at least one, the
corresponding Lie lattice u = pm O, for the relevant endomorphism order O, satisfies [u, u] ⊂ pu.
At odd p, logarithm and exponential converge on these lattices, identify the pth-power map with
multiplication by p, and show that the group is finitely generated, powerful, and torsion free. It is
therefore uniform.

                                                      86
   Give U its lower-p-series valuation. For p ≥ 5 it is saturated and equi-p-valued, with value
denominator e = 1 and graded generators in degree one. The trivial finite free coefficient module Zp
has action in 1 + p EndZp (Zp ). The continuous comparison of [27, Theorem 3.3.3] therefore gives a
cup-compatible isomorphism
                                        ∗
                                      Hcts (U, Zp ) ≃ H ∗ (u, Zp ),
where u is the integral Lie algebra of U . Coefficient naturality and rational agreement in [27,
Theorem 3.1.1(1)–(2)], together with the boundedness of compact rational cochains, identify its
rationalization with Lazard’s comparison for Lie(K) = u[1/p]. Continuous group homomorphisms
preserve lower p-series, so Theorem 3.1.1(3) of the same source gives naturality for restrictions and
for the block maps after passing to such small opens.
    The rational Hochschild–Serre sequence for U ◁ K collapses to finite quotient invariants: positive
cohomology of the finite group K/U vanishes by averaging in Qp . Here Lie(K) is the Lie algebra of
a GLn -form, with n = 2 or 3. The exterior calculation and triviality of its adjoint action are given by
[6, Lemma 3.8.2]. They give generators in degrees 1, 3, . . . , 2n − 1, without choosing explicit cocycles
for all of them. Thus the finite quotient fixes the cohomology, and in particular the low-degree
dimensions are 1, 0, 1 in degrees 1, 2, 3.
    We identify the particular degree-three generator needed here. For the split Lie algebra put
q(X, Y ) = Tr(XY ); for the inner form use reduced trace. Cyclicity gives q([X, Y ], Z) = Tr(X[Y, Z]).
After an algebraically closed splitting-field extension the restriction of q to sln , for n = 2, 3, is nonzero
and nondegenerate. The degree-three invariant-form calculation of [31, Section 11, Theorems 11.1–
11.2] therefore makes its class nonzero. The finite Chevalley–Eilenberg cochain complex commutes
with field extension, so the class already defined on the form over Qp is nonzero. Comparing
restrictions through a common small uniform subgroup shows that the group identifications are
independent of the chosen open subgroup. The upper-left block map restricts the displayed degree-
three matrix trace form to the identical form on the 2 × 2 block, and hence restricts it nontrivially.
    For P3 , choose e3 to have exactly this reduced-trace Lie-algebra class under the natural compact
comparison. The coefficient-field group Γ = Gal(k/Fp ) acts by automorphisms of the Honda division
algebra and preserves reduced trace. Naturality of that comparison therefore makes this normalized
rational class Γ-invariant.
    For the noncompact group J3 , use the reduced affine building in Lemma A.2 with F = Qp . That
lemma supplies a contractible two-dimensional complex on which J3 preserves types, has compact
open face stabilizers fixing their faces pointwise, and has a closed chamber as a fundamental domain.
The cellular resolution and continuous Shapiro give, first with finite coefficients and then with the
derived integral limit,
                                E1a,s =                            a+s
                                          M
                                                  s
                                                Hint (Kσ ) =⇒ Hint     (J3 ).
                                          σ⊂∆
                                        dim σ=a

There are finitely many summands and cellular degrees. After inversion of p, every restriction in a
fixed row identifies with the identity on H s (gl3 , Qp ); changing a face representative only introduces
an inner conjugation. That row is therefore the cellular cochain complex of the simplex ∆ with
these constant coefficients. It has cohomology only in cellular degree zero. The edge map, and then
further restriction to an arbitrary compact open subgroup using intersections, gives the required
assertion for J3 .
    It remains to account for the unipotent radical of D. Fix ℓ and write Q2p = r≥0 p−r Z2p . The
                                                                                        S

two-variable Koszul resolution computes
                                                                   q
                                             −r
                                                                   ^
                                        q
                                      H (p        Z2p , Z/pℓ ) =       (Z/pℓ )2 .


                                                         87
Restriction from stage r + 1 to r multiplies degree q by pq . These systems are pro-zero for q > 0
and constant for q = 0. The restrictions of cochains themselves are surjective: the smaller subgroup
is clopen, so a cochain extends by zero. Thus their inverse limit computes the derived limit, and
the Milnor sequence gives RΓ(Q2p , Z/pℓ ) = Z/pℓ [0]. The calculation remains valid with profinite
parameters, by the same finite Koszul complexes and extension of cochains. Thus this equality holds
for internal derived condensed invariants. The constants map is equivariant for the Levi action, and
its underlying equivalence is therefore an equivariant equivalence. Hochschild–Serre consequently
makes inflation from the Levi subgroup an equivalence at each finite level and then integrally. The
rational Künneth calculation for GL2 (Zp ) × Z× p has degree three equal to the degree-three group of
its first factor, since the second factor has cohomological dimension one and the first has H 2 = 0.
Restriction along A 7→ diag(A, 1) detects the nonzero trace form. This proves (10.3).

10.3    Two presentations of a modification
Write
                           Bk = Spd k,        BC = Spd C ♭ ≃ Spa(C, OC )⋄ .
The chosen embedding F = W (k)[1/p] ⊂ C gives the embedding k ⊂ C ♭ and hence the base map
BC → Bk . All quotient presentations in this subsection are over BC . A determinant lattice on a
slope-zero rank-three bundle is a Zp -lattice in its determinant rational local system. Its frame group
is J3 . Fix a lattice in the one-dimensional rational local system associated to

                                          ∆3 = det(V3 )(−∞).

The action of D3× on it is reduced norm. Its stabilizer is therefore exactly P3 .

Lemma 10.3. Let M classify a form of V3 with P3 -structure and a quotient line at ∞. There are
equivalences
                                M ≃ [P2C /P3 ] ≃ [Ω2C /J3 ].                            (10.4)
The relative classifying maps

                           c3,M : M −→ BBC P3 ,         cJ,M : M −→ BBC J3

record the positive bundle and its lower modification, respectively. For the prescribed finite unramified
field F = W (k)[1/p], these presentations carry a common semilinear arithmetic action of GF over
its action on BC , commuting with both constant groups and inducing the usual action on Ω2C .

Proof. The kernel of a length-one quotient of a form E of V3 is a rank-three bundle E − of degree
zero. If it had a positive Harder–Narasimhan subbundle of rank b < 3 and integral degree d ≥ 1, its
inclusion in E would contradict semistability: d/b ≥ 1/b > 1/3. Thus E − has slope zero. By the
relative slope-zero correspondence it is a rational local system [30, Corollary 8.7.10]; see Lemma 4.4.
    Conversely an upper length-one modification of O3 has, at a geometric point with local parameter
t at ∞, lattice
                                             + 3       + −1 e
                                          (BdR  ) + BdR   t ℓ
for a direction line ℓ. If ℓ lies in a proper rational subspace W , modifying W ⊗ O gives a degree-one
subbundle of rank less than three, which destabilizes. In the other direction, let F ′ be a saturated
destabilizing subbundle of the upper modification, of rank b < 3 and degree d ≥ 1. Its intersection
F ′′ with O3 has degree d − ϵ, where 0 ≤ ϵ ≤ 1. Semistability of O3 forces d = ϵ = 1 and deg F ′′ = 0.
The saturation of F ′′ in O3 has degree at least zero, while semistability forces its degree to be at


                                                   88
most zero. Its torsion quotient therefore has degree zero, so F ′′ is already saturated. Thus F ′′ is a
slope-zero subbundle of O3 , hence equals W ⊗ O for a rational subspace W ; maps between trivial
bundles are matrices over H 0 (O) = Qp . The equality ϵ = 1 says that ℓ ⊂ WC . This proves precisely
the Drinfeld condition.
    Let Z be the sheaf of injections O3 ,→ V3 with length-one cokernel at ∞ preserving the
chosen determinant lattices. The commuting actions are postcomposition by P3 and inverse
precomposition by J3 . The preceding slope calculation and the relative trivial/basic-stratum
theorems [20, Theorems III.2.4 and III.4.5] give actual sheaves of frames in families. They identify
Z/J3 = P2C . In the other direction they identify Z/P3 = Ω2C : the full frame group of the positive
bundle is D3× , and its surjective norm valuation lets one match the determinant lattice locally.
Taking the remaining quotient proves (10.4).
    The arithmetic field is F = W (k)[1/p]; the coefficient field of the curve remains Qp . Let N be the
fixed Honda isocrystal over k, whose period-ring construction gives V3 . All Honda endomorphisms
are defined over k. On the period coefficients tensored over F with N , define the action of γ ∈ GF
by γ ⊗ idN . It commutes with Frobenius and so descends to the bundle. Every element of D3 is
an F -linear endomorphism of N commuting with its Frobenius; its matrix entries are fixed by GF .
Thus this specified descent commutes with the entire D3 -action. We have not trivialized a torsor of
frames of an arbitrary descended basic bundle.
    The untilt ideal ker θ is functorial, so ∆3 inherits descent. The relative slope-zero correspon-
dence makes its rational section space a continuous one-dimensional representation of GF . This
representation need not be trivial. Its valuation character has compact subgroup image in Z, hence
zero, so every lattice in it is stable; no generator is asserted to be invariant. Define the arithmetic
action on an injection u by pullback and the specified descent on source and target. The standard
rational coordinate vectors of O3 are fixed, so

                             γ(guj −1 ) = gγ(u)j −1        (g ∈ P3 , j ∈ J3 ).

The determinant condition is preserved by lattice stability. On the upper side, directions lie in
Q3p ⊗ (O(∞)/O)|∞ . The semilinear scalar in the second factor is common to all coordinates and
cancels in projective space. This is the usual arithmetic action on Ω2C , commuting with J3 . No
arithmetic invariant choice of a frame is required.

Lemma 10.4. The first presentation in (10.4) has a natural arithmetic-equivariant splitting

                                  Hb3 (M) = Hb3 (P3 ) ⊕ Hb1 (P3 )(−1),                            (10.5)

whose first inclusion is the classifying map clc3,M ,b of Lemma 10.1.

Proof. For a profinite set Q = limi Qi with finite Qi , continuity on spatial diamonds and comparison
                               ←−
of finite coefficients give

                         RΓv (P2C × Q, Λℓ ) ≃ colim Map Qi , RΓét (P2C , Λℓ ) .
                                                                                 
                                                  i

Here one applies [43, Proposition 14.9] to P2C × Qi and then [43, Proposition 14.10 and Lemma 15.6];
these statements allow the coefficient ring Λℓ also when its torsion prime is p. The finite-coefficient
cohomology of the analytic projective space agrees with its algebraic cohomology: the proper
comparison of [42, Theorem 3.12], restating [28, Theorem 3.7.2], applies to P2C → Spec C with Fp
coefficients, and the coefficient exact sequences extend it to Λℓ . Thus the projective-space calculation
is valid as the actual condensed geometric complex.


                                                      89
   The anticanonical bundle has canonical projective equivariance, including arithmetic descent,
                                                            −1
and restricts to O(3). Since p ≥ 5, the class hℓ = 3−1 c1 (ωP 2 ) on the quotient restricts to the

The preceding calculation shows that this is an equivalence on every profinite parameter. Apply it

                              2

In its j = 0 component, each bar term sends a continuous constant function to its coefficient-unit

    The projective splitting identifies the classifying image of the P3 trace. We next need to show
that the J3 trace has a nonzero classifying image on the same modification stack. The Drinfeld
cohomology theorem gives arithmetic scalar actions on point-valued cohomology; the following finite
resolution justifies their use on the compact-group descent rows.

Lemma 10.5 (Finite resolutions for geometric coefficients). The complexes CZ are bounded below,
derived solid, and derived p-complete. Let G be a compact p-adic analytic group without p-torsion.
There is a finite projective resolution P• → Zp over ΛG = Zp [[G]], with finitely generated terms and
length dim G, such that, for every derived solid Zp -module complex K with Zp -linear continuous
G-action,
                                     RΓ(G, K)(∗) ≃ HomΛG (P• , K).                              (10.6)
Here Hom is the external complex of morphisms in solid ΛG -modules. A continuous action means an
actual module over Zp [G] in the derived condensed category, rather than an action on K(∗). If K is
bounded below, and an operator γ commuting with G acts by the scalar λj ∈ Qp on H j (K)(∗)[1/p],
it acts by λj on H s (G, H j (K))(∗)[1/p] for every s. For K = CZ the resulting spectral sequence is

                              H s (G, H j (CZ ))(∗)[1/p] =⇒ Hbs+j ([Z/G]),                         (10.7)

with a finite filtration in each total degree.

Proof. Let V = Spa(R, R+ ) be affinoid perfectoid, and write R♭ for its tilt (equal to R in character-
istic p). Its product with Q is affinoid perfectoid with ring Ccts (Q, R) and tilt Ccts (Q, R♭ ). Affinoid
acyclicity gives
                                   RΓv (V × Q, O♭ ) = Ccts (Q, R♭ )[0]
by [43, Proposition 8.8]. Choose a decreasing countable basis of open additive subgroups Ra♭ of the
complete additive group R♭ . Then R♭ = lima (R♭ /Ra♭ )disc is solid. This limit is also the derived limit:
                                          ←−
continuous maps from a profinite set to each discrete quotient have finite image, and lifts of their
finitely many values give surjectivity at each successive quotient. A v-hypercover by disjoint unions
of affinoid perfectoids now expresses the O♭ complex, with all parameters, as a limit of products of


                                                       90
solid modules. Derived solid modules are closed under limits and extensions [48, Theorem 4.4(ii)].
Artin–Schreier gives the assertion for Fp , the coefficient exact sequences give it for Z/pℓ , and the
derived limit gives it for CZ . These complexes are bounded below. The finite coefficient complexes
are derived p-complete, so their limit is too. Evaluation at the point is exact and preserves limits;
in particular
                                     H j (CZ )(∗) = H j (RΓint (Z)).
    The compact-resolution argument in Lemma 6.2 gives Noetherianity of ΛG and projective
dimension dim G for its trivial profinite module, using [50, Sections 1.1–1.2, pp. 275–276] together
with [46, Section 1, Corollary (1)]. Successive finite free presentations therefore give the stated
finite resolution. We give the derived comparison needed to use it. Let C = D(CondZp ) and
D = D(SolidZp ). Solidification is a symmetric monoidal localization L : C → D with fully faithful
right adjoint I [48, Theorem 4.4(ii)]. For the free condensed group ring A = Zp [G], its solidification
LA is ΛG in degree zero, including multiplication [48, Section 3.6 and the proof of Theorem 4.4].
The underived assertion also holds for a free module on any profinite set Q: choose an extremally
disconnected hypercover Q• → Q and solidify its free resolution. The resulting augmented complex
is Zp [[Q• ]] → Zp [[Q]]. Its Pontryagin dual is the augmented complex of continuous Qp /Zp -valued
functions. Discrete coefficients are acyclic on a profinite set, by passage to finite clopen partitions,
so that dual complex is exact. Pontryagin duality and exact condensation prove the assertion.
Functoriality with respect to multiplication on G gives the ring identification.
    For any symmetric monoidal localization there is an induced adjunction

                                  LA : ModA (C) ⇄ ModLA (D) : J,

where J is inclusion followed by restriction along A → I(LA). Its unit has underlying map
M → ILM , and its counit has underlying map LIN → N . The counit is an equivalence; hence J is
fully faithful, with essential image exactly the modules whose underlying complex is derived solid.
This proves the assertion in unbounded degrees. To identify the target, apply this same localization
to the already solid ring B = ΛG . Its essential image inside D(CondB ) consists of precisely the
complexes with derived solid underlying coefficients. By [48, Theorem 4.4], the unbounded category
D(SolidB ) has that same essential image. This identifies the two derived module categories; it is
stronger than an equivalence of their hearts. Equivalently, the comparison is computed on free
modules B[Q] for extremally disconnected profinite Q and their solidifications, which give the
projective resolutions in these categories. Thus the actual condensed action on K belongs to this
category, and full faithfulness identifies its external derived invariants with those computed there.
For geometric K, the action and its coherent group law come from the maps on Z × Q with all
group parameters retained; the preceding solidity argument applies to its underlying complex.
     Condensation preserves the exact profinite resolution [48, Theorem 3.2]. Every Pa is a retract of a
finite free ΛG -module, and HomΛG (ΛG , M ) = M (∗) is exact. Thus these terms are projective against
arbitrary solid coefficient modules and prove (10.6). The complex HomΛG (P• , H j (K)) consists of
retracts of finite sums of H j (K)(∗). Its retracts commute with γ. After exact inversion of p, the
entire complex therefore has scalar action λj , proving the assertion about its cohomology. No
assertion about a condensed sheaf is inferred from its point value.
     Finally the nerve of Z → [Z/G] has terms Z ×Ga . With parameters retained, its descent complex
is precisely the continuous bar construction on CZ . At finite level the equivariant coefficient unit
Λℓ → CZ,ℓ is the evaluation map of Lemma 10.1 on every profinite parameter. Its map on G-invariants
is therefore exactly uZ,G,ℓ , since their degree-a bar maps agree. This identity also holds after the
derived coefficient limit and inversion of p. Its source after that limit is RΓ(G, Zp )(∗) ≃ RΓint (G):
the finite reductions of constants are surjective on every profinite parameter, and internal invariants

                                                  91
and point evaluation preserve limits. The resolution bounds the group direction by dim G, giving
(10.7) and the asserted finite filtration before and after rationalization.
Lemma 10.6. On M, abbreviate the classifying images clc3,M ,b (e3 ) and clcJ,M ,b (eJ ) by e3 and eJ .
Both are nonzero and satisfy e3 = κeJ for some κ ∈ Q×
                                                    p . We retain this abbreviation for subsequent
pullbacks from M.
Proof. By (10.5), the class e3 is nonzero. Arithmetic acts trivially on its first summand and by
χ−1
  cyc on the second. The cyclotomic character of GF has open image, so the invariant subspace is
precisely Qp e3 .
     Choose a compact uniform open subgroup U ⊂ J3 . The restriction resU (eJ ) of the source
group class eJ ∈ Hb3 (J3 ) is nonzero by Lemma 10.2. Let qU : [Ω2C /U ] → M be the quotient
map and cU : [Ω2C /U ] → BBC U its relative classifying map. Extension of torsor structure gives
cJ,M qU ≃ (BBC (U ,→ J3 ))cU . Thus Lemma 10.1 identifies the restriction of the geometric eJ with
clcU ,b (resU (eJ )). Apply (10.7) to [Ω2C /U ]. The integral Drinfeld computation [16, Theorems 1.1
and 5.1] identifies H j (CΩ2 )(∗)[1/p] as a representation on which arithmetic acts by the scalar χ−j  cyc ,
                            C
with j = 0, 1, 2 and no higher rows. Its integral convention is the derived limit of finite p-power
coefficients followed by inversion of p, exactly the convention here: the finite reduction tower defines
b p on the pro-etale site, and derived global sections preserve its derived inverse limit. The calculation
Z
is for hyperplanes rational over the ground field, here Qp ; we merely restrict its arithmetic action to
GF .
     In degree zero the same computation gives a one-dimensional rational point value. The finite
coefficient units form a map η : Zp → CΩ2 after their derived limit: on every profinite parameter
                                              C
the finite reductions of constants are surjective, so their limit is Zp . Pullback to any geometric point
sends the unit to 1. Hence
                                                     ≃
                               H 0 (η)(∗)[1/p] : Qp −−→ H 0 (CΩ2 )(∗)[1/p].
                                                                  C

For each finite projective term Pa in the U -resolution of Lemma 10.5, applying HomΛU (Pa , −) gives
a retract of a finite sum of these point maps, and the unit commutes with its defining idempotent.
It is therefore an isomorphism after inversion of p in every term. Naturality of the descent spectral
sequence for η maps its single constant row to the bottom row of geometric descent. By the compact
bar identity, the E23,0 bottom-row map of the spectral-sequence morphism induced by the finite
units defining clcU ,b is the resulting isomorphism
                                           ≃
                                 Hb3 (U ) −−→ H 3 (U, H 0 (CΩ2 ))(∗)[1/p].
                                                              C

It sends resU (eJ ) to the nonzero bottom-row class. This argument uses the rational point value and
the natural finite projective complex.
     By Lemma 10.5, an element γ ∈ GF with cyclotomic character of infinite order acts on the
actual jth descent row by this scalar. The only possible incoming differentials to (3, 0) are from
(1, 1) and (0, 2). Their source scalars differ from 1, so both differentials vanish. There is no outgoing
differential from the bottom row. Thus the nonzero bottom-row image of resU (eJ ) survives in
Hb3 ([Ω2C /U ]), proving that the geometric class eJ on M is nonzero. The map to BBC J3 is arithmetic
equivariant, so this class is invariant and belongs to the line Qp e3 . This proves the assertion.

Apply the relative slope-zero equivalence on the pro-etale site of a perfectoid test object over Y1,C .
To verify exactness without assuming it, take a common pro-etale cover trivializing the three rational
local systems. Full faithfulness identifies the maps with matrices of sections of Qp . Write the
quotient map as (b1 , b2 , b3 ). The open loci bi ̸= 0 cover, since its rank is one on every geometric

j ̸= i. The given map from L2 ⊗ Qp to this kernel is a square matrix invertible on every geometric

systems is exact and locally split.

quotient by det L2 is an integral lattice on the quotient line. Refine the cover to choose an integral

The first remembers the same structured positive bundle. The second forgets the adapted filtration

Lemma 10.9 (Origin in the rational sphere). The reduced-trace class in Hb3 (P3 ), after the constant
coefficient map and coefficient-field descent, is the degree-three class of an element α3 ∈ π−3 L0 T .
This element is nonzero and its image in π−3 L0 LK(1) T is nonzero.

Proof. Reduction followed by the ordinary coefficient localization E3,0 (k) → Dℓ sends the integral
constant class chosen in Lemma 10.8 to its specified finite classes dℓ . These have unbounded p-power
order and are compatible with ordinary reduction. Hence the coefficient image of the rational trace
in H 3 (P3 , E3,0 (k))[1/p] is nonzero. The trace construction and its coefficient map commute with
the semilinear finite Galois action; their class is invariant and descends by Lemma 3.2.
    We explain why this cohomology class is an actual rational homotopy class. A central element
z = 1 + p acts trivially on E3,0 (k) and by the nontrivial scalar z −q on the nonzero periodicity
twist E3,2q (k), for q ̸= 0. This is the action on ω ⊗q in the contravariant deformation convention of
Lemma 4.9. A central group element acts as the identity on group cohomology when its coefficient
action is combined with its trivial inner conjugation. Equivalently, the natural transformation
furnished by that element gives a homotopy to the identity on the bar resolution. Thus z −q − 1
annihilates H s (P3 , E3,2q (k)). It is a nonzero element of Zp , so every such row vanishes after inversion
of p.
    The descent of Proposition 7.2 is exact and strongly convergent. The finite stabilizer resolutions
and exact semilinear descent of Lemmas 3.2 and 10.5 bound its cohomological amplitude. Ratio-
nalization therefore preserves its finite filtrations. Only internal degree zero remains. After finite
coefficient-field descent, the actual coefficient maps fit into the commutative square

                       3 (G (k), E (k))[1/p]                   3 (G (k), D ) [1/p]
                                                                               
                      Hcts 3      3,0                    limℓ Hcts 3      ℓ
                                                         ←−
                                    ≃                                  ≃

                               π−3 L0 T                   π−3 L0 holimℓ L1 (T /pℓ ).

The upper arrow is induced by the coefficient reductions and localizations just described. The
left isomorphism is the single-row rational descent calculation. The right isomorphism uses the
natural, unique (s, t) = (3, 0) edge of Lemma 9.5 and the vanishing of the Milnor lim1 term in
Proposition 9.7. The finite-length model identifies those coefficient maps with the actual Moore
quotient and localization maps on every descent term. For commutativity, first choose an integral
representative of a p-power multiple of a rational source cohomology class. Finite source filtration
and rational collapse allow a further p-power multiple to survive to homotopy. Naturality in
Proposition 7.2 identifies its finite target edges with that same multiple of the coefficient images, for
every ℓ. This multiplier is chosen in the source, independently of ℓ. Taking the coefficient limit and
then inverting p gives the displayed square.
    By Lemma 9.6, the bottom map is the rationalization of T → LK(1) T . The nonzero rational
trace image in the upper left therefore determines α3 ∈ π−3 L0 T , and its image in the upper right
is the nonzero rational class of the compatible finite constants. The square proves the asserted
nonvanishing of its actual localization without an additional arithmetic coefficient splitting.

Theorem 10.10 (Rational compatibility). The generators in (3.3) may be chosen so that α1 = ζ
and the actual localization map T → LK(1) T satisfies

in degrees 0, −1, −3, −4, −5, −6, −8, −9, respectively.

Proof. The rational exterior calculation (3.3) makes degree −3 one-dimensional. Choose its generator
to be the nonzero constant-origin class of Lemma 10.9. The determinant class is the degree-one
group primitive by its construction in Section 7, and its actual image is nonzero rationally by

image gives α3 7→ cδ with c ̸= 0. All coefficient comparisons and localizations used above respect

gives the second formula with the same scalar. The exterior calculation then supplies the displayed
basis.


11     Applying fracture to the height-three overlap
The coefficient arguments have now produced the maps required by the finite construction of
Proposition 2.2. We apply it with one choice of local maps and compatibility homotopy, obtaining
both the filtration and its attachment identification. The local basis results used here were proved
without the rational-obstruction theorem, which enters only in the final subsection.

                                                      98
11.2     The first middle attachment
The filtration has been constructed without a nonvanishing assumption. We now ask whether its first
height-one layer attaches nontrivially. The answer depends on the actual canonical K(2)-localization
map, not on the rational dimensions of the layers.
    First note why rationalizing the filtration does not answer this question. Its rationalized cofibers
are single Q-modules in the strictly decreasing degrees 0, −1, −3, −4, −5, −6, −8, −9. Inductively, if
the previous rationalized boundaries vanish, L0 Fi−1 is the sum of the earlier shifts of Q. The degree
of the new cofiber is smaller than every earlier degree, and hence cannot be a homotopy degree of
ΣL0 Fi−1 . A Q-module map from the new shift to that suspension is zero. This proves by induction
that every rationalized boundary vanishes. A spectral boundary can nevertheless be nonzero.

11.2.1    A conditional lifting criterion
Let
                                      uX : L0 X −→ L0 LK(2) X
be induced by the canonical localization unit. Retain the specific h : Umid → Y , β3 , and d3 = ∂w h|U3
constructed in Section 11, where U3 = Σ−3 L1 S.

Proposition 11.2 (Canonical-map criterion). If uX does not kill β3 ∈ π−3 L0 X, then

                                     d3 : U3 = Σ−3 L1 S −→ ΣW

is nonzero as a map of underlying spectra.

Proof. Suppose d3 were null after forgetting the module structure. Applying spectral mapping
spaces from U3 to the cofiber sequence
                                                  q w   ∂
                                       W −→ X −
                                              → Y −−→ ΣW

shows that the chosen nullhomotopy lifts h|U3 to a map e  h3 : U3 → X. Rationally, L0 W has homotopy
only in degrees 0 and −1. Its groups in degrees −3 and −4 are zero, so the cofiber sequence makes
π−3 L0 X → π−3 L0 Y an isomorphism. The construction of h sends the degree-−3 generator of L0 U3
to the projection of β3 . Thus L0 e
                                  h3 sends it to β3 itself. This uses only the image of that underlying
homotopy element.
    But LK(2) U3 = 0, since U3 is E(1)-local. By localization adjunction, every map from U3 to the
K(2)-local target LK(2) X is null. In particular, the composite of h  e 3 with the unit X → L
                                                                                               K(2) X is
null. Rationalizing and using naturality would make uX kill β3 , contrary to the hypothesis.

    This criterion is independent of the existence argument: its antecedent was not used to construct
h, the stages, or their terminal equivalence.

11.2.2    The companion canonical-map theorem
For every prime p ≥ 5, the canonical map for the uncompleted sphere S,

                                    L0 LK(3) S −→ L0 LK(2) LK(3) S,                               (11.5)

is nonzero on π−3 . This is [39, Theorem 1.1].



                                                  99
Corollary 11.3 (Nonzero first middle attachment). For every prime p ≥ 5, retain the common
height-three choice in Section 11, which realizes Theorem 1.1 and the height-three instance of
Theorem 2.3. Then
                               d3 : Σ−3 L1 S −→ Σ L2 S ∨ Σ−1 L2 S
                                                                  

is nonzero as a map of underlying spectra.
Proof. Set T0 = LK(3) S. The completion map S → S is a mod-p equivalence. After p-localization its
cofiber has invertible multiplication by p, hence is rational and K(3)-acyclic. Completion therefore
                                ≃
induces an equivalence ec : T0 −→ T . Naturality of the K(2)-localization unit gives

                                    L0 T0             L0 LK(2) T0
                                  L0 ec ≃                    ≃ L0 LK(2) ec                    (11.6)
                                    L0 T              L0 LK(2) T.

The top map is nonzero on π−3 by [39, Theorem 1.1]. Hence so is the bottom map.
   Let ℓ : T → X = L2 T be the localization map. Its cofiber is E(2)-acyclic. Since ⟨E(2)⟩ =
⟨K(0) ∨ K(1) ∨ K(2)⟩, that cofiber is both rationally and K(2)-acyclic. The vertical maps in the
next naturality square are therefore equivalences:

                                    L0 T                  L0 LK(2) T
                                   L0 ℓ ≃                    ≃ L0 LK(2) ℓ                     (11.7)
                                               uX
                                    L0 X              L0 LK(2) X.

Thus uX is nonzero on π−3 , and it is the same canonical map under these identifications. An
arbitrary nonzero map between the same two objects would not suffice.
    The square (11.7) is a square of S-modules by (2.1). Rationalization is extension to Q = HQp ,
so its homotopy maps are Qp -linear. Equivalently, the action of π0 S = Zp on rational homotopy
extends uniquely across inversion of p. By Theorem 10.10,

                                            π−3 L0 X = Qp α3 .

A nonzero Qp -linear map from this one-dimensional space cannot kill α3 or its nonzero scalar
multiple β3 = c−1 α3 . This conclusion requires no numerical identification of a primitive chosen in
the companion proof with the chosen α3 .
   The hypothesis of Proposition 11.2 is now satisfied, so d3 ̸= 0.

   Thus the companion theorem enters only in verifying the antecedent of the conditional criterion.
The stages and local bases in Theorem 1.1 were constructed independently.


A     The rational boundary
We give the height-three argument of [6, Sections 2.5–2.6 and 3.9–6.3] for the rational algebra used
in Theorem 10.10. The separate trace calculation in Section 10 identifies the localization of its
degree-three generator.
Proposition A.1 (The rational height-three sphere). For every prime p ≥ 5, there is an isomorphism
of graded algebras
                           π∗ L0 T ≃ ΛQp (α1 , α3 , α5 ),  |αi | = −i.

                                                    100
    The proof reduces this homotopy calculation to a comparison with constant coefficients. Put

                             W = W (Fp ),           K̆ = W [1/p],
                              A = W [[u1 , u2 ]],   G = P3 ⋊ Gal(Fp /Fp ).

The map to be proved an isomorphism is the constants inclusion

                                 H ∗ (G, W )[1/p] −→ H ∗ (G, A)[1/p].                           (A.1)

The compact Lie-algebra calculation identifies the source with the exterior algebra in cohomological
degrees 1, 3, 5. Comparisons of the Lubin–Tate and Drinfeld towers identify the target with the same
graded vector space after adjoining to both sides an exterior class ϵ of degree one. A continuous
equivariant additive retraction onto W makes the source a direct summand. Comparing dimensions,
including the common ϵ factor, then forces the complementary cohomology to vanish. Since the
original constants inclusion is multiplicative, this proves the algebra comparison. Rational Morava
descent converts it to the asserted homotopy algebra.
    The tower comparisons must be made integrally: the arithmetic estimates give torsion bounds
uniform over the charts and radii, so that the derived limits and group invariants can be taken
before inverting p. We first establish the Drinfeld model’s actual scalar equivalence. Its building
calculation also supplies the properties used for the group J3 in Lemma 10.2.

A.1    The Drinfeld model and its building
Lemma A.2 (The reduced rank-three building). Let F be a non-Archimedean local field with
normalized valuation ν, valuation ring O, uniformizer π, and residue field κ of cardinality q. Let B
be the reduced affine building of GL3 (F ), equivalently the building of PGL3 (F ), and put

                               J3 (F ) = {g ∈ GL3 (F ) : ν(det g) = 0}.

Then B is a contractible, locally finite, two-dimensional simplicial complex. Each vertex has
2(q 2 + q + 1) adjacent vertices. The group J3 (F ) preserves vertex types. The setwise stabilizer of
every nonempty face is compact open in J3 (F ) and fixes that face pointwise. Any closed chamber is
a fundamental domain, including all its faces.
Proof. For GL3 , the semisimple quotient used to define the reduced building is the split group
PGL3 of rank two. The geometry statement in [37, Section 1.1.2, p. 6] applies over the local field F :
the reduced building is locally finite, has dimension the rank of this quotient, and is equivariantly
contractible for every compact subgroup of the quotient. Taking the trivial compact subgroup gives
contractibility.
   In the lattice-chain model of [17, Section 5.6, pp. 952–953], vertices are homothety classes [L] of
O-lattices in F 3 , and a chamber can be represented by a strict cyclic chain

                                        L0 ⊃ L1 ⊃ L2 ⊃ πL0 .

Modulo πL0 this is a complete flag in κ3 . Thus a chamber has three vertices and is a two-simplex.
An adjacent vertex to [L] has a unique representative M with πL ⊊ M ⊊ L. These representatives
correspond to the nonzero proper subspaces of L/πL ∼   = κ3 . There are q 2 + q + 1 lines and the same
number of planes, giving the stated valency and a direct local-finiteness check. Since the complex is
locally finite, its metric polyhedral and weak simplicial topologies agree locally on finite unions of
simplices. Its realization is therefore a contractible CW complex. The augmented cellular chain
complex over Z is exact, with free direct-sum terms in degrees 0, 1, 2.

                                                    101
   Fix the standard lattice O3 and define

                                  type([gO3 ]) = ν(det g)   (mod 3).

Changing an integral basis changes the determinant by a unit; scalar homothety changes its valuation
by a multiple of three. This type is therefore well defined, and J3 (F ) preserves it. The three vertices
of a chamber have all three types, since successive flag steps change the lattice determinant valuation
by one.
    To send the standard chamber to a given chamber, start at the latter’s unique type-zero vertex.
Write its lattice as M = gO3 with ν(det g) = 3m, and replace M by M0 = π −m M . Reanchor the
cyclic chain at M0 . A basis f1 , f2 , f3 of M0 adapted to its residue flag gives

                     Mi = πOf1 + · · · + πOfi + Ofi+1 + · · · + Of3     (i = 1, 2).

The map from the standard basis to (f1 , f2 , f3 ) has determinant valuation zero, because its image
lattice is M0 . It lies in J3 (F ) and sends the standard chamber to the given chamber.
    If h ∈ J3 (F ) stabilizes [L], then hL = aL for some a ∈ F × . Taking determinant valuations
relative to L gives 0 = ν(det h) = 3ν(a), so a is a unit and hL = L. Hence the vertex stabilizer is
exactly AutO (L), a conjugate of the compact open group GL3 (O). A setwise face stabilizer fixes
each vertex because their types are distinct. It is therefore the finite intersection of these vertex
stabilizers, hence is compact open and fixes the face pointwise.
    Every face lies in a chamber, and chamber transitivity sends it into the standard chamber. Its
set of vertex types picks out a unique face there. Type preservation also preserves the barycentric
coordinates labeled by those types, so no distinct points of the closed chamber are identified. This
proves the fundamental-domain assertion, including its faces.

Lemma A.3 (The structure sheaf of the height-three Drinfeld model). Let H be the p-adic completion
of the standard separated strictly semistable weak formal model of Ω2Qp over Zp . The canonical scalar
map
                                           Zp −→ RΓ(H, O)
is an equivalence of condensed complexes, and is equivariant for the action of GL3 (Zp ). Here the
right side retains the derived sections on H × Q for every profinite set Q.
Proof. The imported model facts are given in [22, Sections 3.6, 3.8, 6.1–6.2, 6.4 and 7.1, and the
proof of Proposition 6.5]. After ordinary p-adic completion the local strictly semistable equations and
the special fiber are unchanged. The model is separated: each of the increasing weak-formal opens
lies in a separated blowup stage, and any two lie in one common such stage; the local closed-diagonal
criterion is preserved by completion. The components and their nonempty intersections are products
of successive blowups of projective spaces along rational linear subspaces. They are indexed by the
vertices and simplices of the locally finite two-dimensional Bruhat–Tits building B of Lemma A.2.
Only the factors in dimensions 0, 1, 2 occur here.
    We compute the structure-sheaf cohomology of exactly these factors. For Pnk , 0 ≤ n ≤ 2, over a
field k, use the standard affine cover. On an intersection indexed by a nonempty set I ⊂ {0, . . . , n},
                                                                                              P
its degree-zero homogeneous Laurent monomials have exponent vector (a0 , . . . , an ) with ai = 0
and ai ≥ 0 when i ∈ / I. The Čech complex decomposes by these monomials. The constant monomial
gives the cochain complex of the full simplex and hence k in degree zero. For any other monomial
its negative support N = {i : ai < 0} is nonempty and proper, and the monomial occurs exactly for
I ⊃ N . Adding and deleting a fixed index outside N , with the usual alternating sign, contracts this
summand. It follows that
                                           RΓ(Pnk , O) = k[0].

                                                  102
    The only nontrivial blowups needed for a surface factor are blowups at the specified smooth
rational points. The calculation for one such point is local on the surface and can be made in étale
coordinates (u, v). For the blowup of A2k at the origin, the two standard charts and their overlap
have rings

             Bu = k[u, t],            Bv = k[v, s],            Buv = k[u, t, t−1 ],               v = ut,       s = t−1 .

In the common Laurent ring a monomial ua tb , with a ≥ 0 and b ∈ Z, belongs to Bu when b ≥ 0
and to Bv when b ≤ a. These two conditions cover all monomials. Their intersection is spanned by
0 ≤ b ≤ a, where ua tb = ua−b v b . Thus the affine Čech sequence

                                     0 −→ k[u, v] −→ Bu ⊕ Bv −→ Buv −→ 0

is exact. The same sequence remains exact after localization and flat étale base change, which are
the local coordinate changes in question. The affine chart calculation therefore proves Rπ∗ O = O
for each required smooth-point blowup π. Blowing up an invertible ideal is an isomorphism. This
covers the later strict transforms of rational lines on the surface and the point centers on a projective
line, all of which are Cartier divisors. Iterating the result, and using the finite affine-cover double
Čech complex for products over k, proves

                                                       RΓ(Y, OY ) = k[0]

for every stratum Y occurring in this height-three model. This argument makes no assertion
about other rational varieties. For a profinite parameter Q the same calculations give Ccts (Q, k)[0]:
continuous maps to each discrete section module factor through a finite clopen partition of Q, and
the filtered union of the resulting finite powers preserves the exact Čech sequences.
    We next justify the resolution by intersections of components. On a standard special-fiber chart
its augmented form is the alternating complex
                                                M                                  M
        k[t0 , . . . , td ]/(t0 · · · tr ) −→       k[t0 , . . . , td ]/(ti ) −→         k[t0 , . . . , td ]/(ti , tj ) −→ · · · .
                                                i                                  i<j

For a nonzero monomial, let Z be the nonempty set of indices i ≤ r at which its exponent is zero. Its
coefficient complex is the augmented cochain complex of the simplex on Z, so it is exact. This proves
exactness monomial by monomial. The calculation is unchanged by the torus variables, localizations,
and flat étale base changes in the semistable charts. Local finiteness of the components now gives
the global resolution by the products of their intersection sheaves. Taking derived global sections
with a profinite parameter and using the preceding stratum calculation computes RΓ(HFp × Q, O)
by the three product terms below. The arrow from Ccts (Q, Fp ) is the separate canonical constants
augmentation:
                                      Y                             Y                               Y
              Ccts (Q, Fp ) −→             Ccts (Q, Fp ) −→               Ccts (Q, Fp ) −→                Ccts (Q, Fp ),
                                    σ∈B0                           σ∈B1                            σ∈B2

with the cellular incidence signs. Products occur because the strata are indexed by all cells of the
building.
    Here is a contraction valid for these product cochains. By Lemma A.2, the augmented bounded
cellular chain complex C• (B; Z) → Z is exact. Its terms are free abelian, and each cycle subgroup is
free because it is a subgroup of a free abelian group. The short exact sequences of cycles therefore
split. Choosing splittings gives a chain contraction of the augmented complex. This contraction
is not asserted to be equivariant. Each basis cell maps to a finite cellular chain, since a cellular

                                                                103
chain group is a direct sum of its cells. Dualizing into Ccts (Q, Fp ) consequently gives a contraction
of the displayed product cochains: every output coordinate is a finite linear combination of input
coordinates and is continuous for the product topology. The contraction is natural in Q.
    The canonical constants map into cellular cochains is equivariant for the building action. The
contraction proves that its underlying condensed map is an equivalence; the forgetful functor from
equivariant derived objects is conservative, so this same canonical map is an equivariant equivalence.
In particular
                                              ≃
                                         Fp −−→ RΓ(HFp , O).
Finally the formal structure sheaf is derived p-complete, and derived global sections preserve its
inverse limit of reductions. The cone of the actual scalar map Zp → RΓ(H, O) is therefore derived
p-complete. Flatness of the semistable model identifies its reduction modulo p with the zero cone
just computed. Every successive reduction modulo pℓ is then zero, and derived p-completeness
makes the cone itself zero. This proves the claimed equivariant equivalence.

A.2    The rational boundary calculation
Proof of Proposition A.1. All cohomology complexes below retain their condensed parameters; all
inverse limits and group invariants are derived and taken before inverting p.
1. Arithmetic bounds. Let K be complete discretely valued of mixed characteristic (0, p) with
perfect residue field, and let C be a completed algebraic closure. The scalar class obtained from the
cyclotomic logarithm gives a natural map

                           OK [ϵ] −→ RΓ(GK , OC ),                     |ϵ| = 1,   ϵ2 = 0.

Its cone is cohomologically nonnegative, and in each degree its cohomology is killed by a power of p.
The required bounds can be chosen uniformly after finite tame extensions of K. For the finitely
many nonzero twists j = 1, 2, the same assertion of bounded torsion holds for H i (GK , OC (−j)),
with H 0 = 0. Here is the part of the proof that gives uniformity, [6, Sections 4.2–4.4]. Choose a
finite cyclotomic stage B/K far enough out that the remaining Zp -tower is totally ramified and
sufficiently ramified in the sense of [6, Definition 4.2.5 and Lemma 4.2.6], generated by σ, with χ(σ)
a generator of 1 + ps Zp , s ≥ 2. Write M = OK     b∞ and V = ker(t : K∞ → B) for normalized trace.
                                                                       c
The estimates in [6, (4.2.9), (4.2.15), Lemmas 4.2.12 and 4.2.17, and the proof of Proposition 4.2.18]
are
                         ∥t∥ ≤ |p|−1/(p−1) ,     ∥(σ − 1)−1 ∥V ≤ |p|−1−1/[p(p−1)] .
For existence of the inverse, at a finite cyclic level σ − 1 has kernel B and image the trace-zero
subspace. Its inverse there is bounded by the second estimate, so the inverses extend compatibly to
the completion. The estimate follows by induction from normalized layer trace tn = p−1 Tr and

Set M0 = OB ⊕ (V ∩ M ) ⊂ M . The first norm bound gives pM ⊂ M0 , so M/M0 is killed by
p. For j ∈ {−2, −1, 1, 2}, put λ = χ(σ)−j ; then vp (λ − 1) = s, since p ≥ 5. The inequality
|λ − 1| ∥(σ − 1)−1 ∥V < 1 follows from s ≥ 2 > 1 + 1/[p(p − 1)]. A Neumann series therefore
inverts σ − λ on V with the same bound [6, Lemma 4.4.1], so its cokernel on V ∩ M is killed by
p2 . On OB its cokernel is killed by ps . Thus H 1 (⟨σ⟩, M0 (j)) is killed by ps , while the invariants
vanish. The sequence M0 → M → M/M0 gives a ps+1 bound for H 1 (⟨σ⟩, M (j)). The rational


                                                      104
decomposition B ⊕V also shows directly that M (j) has no invariants under the tail. Restriction from
Gal(K∞ /K) to this open tail injects H 1 , because the tail has no invariants. The cyclotomic fixed-
field calculation and affinoid perfectoid integral acyclicity compare this with OC (j) [6, Lemmas 4.3.2
and 4.3.5]. Before cyclotomic invariants, the perfectoid-tail degree-zero comparison has almost-zero
cohomological error. The maximal ideal of the completed valuation ring is flat and idempotent, so
this error is annihilated by p in the equivariant derived category. In the resulting triangle after
invariants, the error Y has H 0 (Y ) = 0 and pH i (Y ) = 0 for i > 0 [6, Lemma 4.3.5 and Theorem 4.4.3].
This gives the conservative bound ps+2 in degree one; the remaining degrees are bounded as in the
same triangle. The untwisted calculation retains the constant summand and its cyclotomic-logarithm
class, giving the displayed map OK [ϵ] and its bounded-torsion cone. Sufficient ramification persists
under tame base change. For a finite tame extension L/K, let e = e(LB/B) be the effective tame
ramification index after the chosen initial cyclotomic stage B. If dn is the different exponent of a
p-layer above B, then the base-changed exponent is

                                           d′n = edn − (e − 1)(p − 1).

Consequently the same tail, s, and bounds work for all the tame extensions in question. Only
existence of these tame-uniform bounds is used; the sharp numerical constants in the preprint are
unnecessary.
2. Semistable comparison.
First apply this to a small affine semistable formal OK -scheme U = Spf(R0 ) of relative dimension
d ≤ 2, where small means admitting an étale morphism to a standard semistable chart. Write U for
its generic fiber, UC for its completed base change to OC , and UC for the geometric generic fiber. Use
the divisorial log structure and the valuation log structure on the base. The semistable logarithmic
comparison of [14, Sections 1.5–1.6 and Theorem 4.11], extending the smooth comparison of [12,
Theorem 8.3], applies on étale charts
                                          ±1
                   OC ⟨T0 , . . . , Tr , Tr+1 , . . . , Td±1 ⟩/(T0 · · · Tr − a),   0 ̸= a ∈ mC .

They identify the cohomology of Lηζp −1 Rν∗ O b + with Ωj
                                                          UC ,log {−j} for 0 ≤ j ≤ d. No properness is
required for these local statements. The logarithmic differentials on U are coherent and flat over
OK , and their higher cohomology on this affine vanishes. Their completed base changes are the
displayed differentials on UC . The needed completed projection formula can be checked modulo
p: a flat module modulo p is free over the artinian local ring OK /p, and continuous cochains on a
compact group commute with these direct sums, since a map to a discrete direct sum has finite
image. Derived p-completeness then proves the projection formula before reduction. For j > 0,
the normalized map from the Breuil–Kisin twist to the Tate twist has cokernel killed by p, so the
preceding arithmetic bounds apply to each of the finitely many positive graded pieces.
    For the comparison with the original complex, put CU = RΓ(UC,proét , O    b + ), I = (ζp − 1) ⊂ OC ,
and AU = LηI CU . The ideal I is GK -stable, although its displayed generator need not be fixed. On
the semistable charts above, the perfectoid-cover edge map of [14, Section 3.3, Equation (3.3.1)]
has almost-zero cone. Its source is the continuous cochain complex for ∆ ≃ Zdp , represented by a
d-variable Koszul complex [14, Lemma 3.7]. Thus H q (CU ) is almost zero for q > d. Moreover, [14,
Theorems 3.9 and 4.11] identifies AU with a complex concentrated in degrees [0, d]. The degree-zero
cohomology of CU is I-torsion-free; indeed, it is the formal structure ring [14, Proposition 4.4].
    Set BU = τ ≤d CU . Lemma 6.9 of [12], together with compatibility of LηI with truncation, gives
maps
                              a : AU −→ BU ,      b : I ⊗d ⊗ BU −→ AU

                                                           105
whose composites are the natural ideal inclusions. The invariant element pd ∈ I d gives an equivariant
map OC → I ⊗d ; composing it with b gives bp : BU → AU with abp = pd and bp a = pd . Hence the
kernel and cokernel of each H q (a) are killed by pd , and the long exact sequence gives p2d H q (cone(a)) =
0. The triangle
                               cone(a) −→ cone(AU → CU ) −→ τ >d CU
then shows that p2d+1 kills every cohomology group of the full cone: the last term has almost-zero
cohomology, which is killed by p. The full cone is connective. This argument concerns its cohomology
and does not assert that it carries a strict Z/p2d+1 -module structure.
    After GK -invariants, the first-quadrant spectral sequence has terms H s (GK , H t (cone(AU →
CU ))). In total degree k its filtration has at most k + 1 pieces, each killed by p2d+1 . Therefore
p(2d+1)(k+1) kills the cohomology in that degree. These conservative bounds depend on d and k,
and are independent of the chart and its tame extension. The positive logarithmic graded pieces
retain the arithmetic bounds proved in Step 1. Tame descent preserves these bounds, by unramified
semilinear descent and averaging over tame inertia. We obtain the natural comparison
                                                              b+)
                                      O(U)[ϵ] −→ RΓ(Uproét , O

with nonnegative cone and degreewise bounds independent of the chosen charts and their tame
extensions. For a general semistable affine, choose an étale hypercover refining the Čech nerve of a
small-chart cover, with each term a finite disjoint union of small affine charts. Such finite covers
of the matching objects exist because the base is quasi-compact and quasi-separated. Apply étale
hyperdescent to the actual natural comparison map. Its levelwise cones are connective and have the
uniform degreewise bounds just proved. In a fixed total degree the descent spectral sequence has
only finitely many cosimplicial and cohomological positions, so the same bounded-torsion conclusion
follows. Gluing next by a separated affine formal cover likewise preserves a bound in every total
degree, since the relevant Čech diagonal is finite. This proves the part of [6, Sections 5.3–5.6] needed
here.
3. The two global calculations. The Lubin–Tate space LTK̆ is the open two-dimensional ball. Exhaust
it by closed balls of radii |p|1/ℓ for primes ℓ ̸= p; each has a smooth model after a tame extension.
The two-index system of integral functions modulo pj , indexed by radius and j, is Mittag–Leffler.
This coefficient limit is taken over the fixed base K̆, whose uniformizer is p; the tame extensions
were used for the local comparisons. More generally, with uniformizer ϖ, for a fixed inner radius r
and reduction exponent j take M = ⌈(j + 1)/ log|ϖ| (r)⌉. Restriction from any s ≥ r1/(j+1) , with
                                 ′
source reduced modulo any ϖj for j ′ ≥ j, kills modulo ϖj all terms of total degree at least M , and
the surviving coefficients are integral. Conversely every monomial of degree less than M is already
integral on the larger ball. The image is therefore the fixed module spanned by those monomials.
Continuous functions from a profinite parameter preserve this image statement by finite clopen
partitions. A cofinal diagonal reduces the two indices to a countable Mittag–Leffler tower. Thus
the derived two-index limit is A in degree zero, as in [6, Lemmas 6.1.2–6.1.3]. The preceding local
comparisons and the countable inverse-limit spectral sequence give
                                                               b+)
                                      A[ϵ] −→ RΓ(LTK̆,proét , O

with nonnegative, degreewise bounded-torsion cone: in any degree the limit spectral sequence uses
only a limit in that degree and a lim1 from the preceding degree, both with bounds uniform over
the radii. No uniform bound over all cohomological degrees is needed.
   The other space is Ω2Qp , with the formal model H of Lemma A.3. That lemma gives the actual
equivariant scalar equivalence Zp ≃ RΓ(H, O), including all profinite parameters. It supplies the

                                                    106
structure-sheaf assertion needed from [6, Theorem 6.2.1]. The semistable comparison now gives

                                      Zp [ϵ] −→ RΓ(Ω2Qp ,proét , O
                                                                  b+)

with the same degreewise bounded-torsion conclusion.
4. The tower comparison. The bridge between these calculations is an equivalence of families,
not merely a bijection of geometric points. The relative Rapoport–Zink/shtuka comparison [45,
Theorem 24.2.5 and its proof, pp. 227–229], using the family comparison of its Theorem 22.3.1,
identifies the universal integral Tate local system with that in a modification

                                 0 −→ T ⊗Zp O −→ E −→ i∗ L −→ 0,

where T = Tp (X ) is the actual integral Tate local system of the universal p-divisible group X , of
rank three, and L is a line at the untilt divisor, and E is fiberwise V3 . The relative basic-stratum
frame torsors [20, Theorems III.2.4 and III.4.5] identify the two quotient presentations of this
moduli problem. Honda endomorphisms are already defined over Fp3 ; their canonical action and
the basic-stratum equivalence descend from the geometric residue field as in Lemma 4.4. After the
height-component normalization on the Lubin–Tate side, this gives

                                     [LT⋄K̆ /G] ≃ [Ω2,⋄
                                                    Qp / GL3 (Zp )].

Pulling back the two atlases gives a common space with commuting pro-étale torsor actions. Their
Čech nerves therefore compute the same integral condensed cohomology object; this is the actual
descent argument behind [6, Theorem 3.9.1 and (3.9.3)]. Taking invariants of the comparison cones
remains harmless: they are nonnegative, so each diagonal of the group-cohomology spectral sequence
contains finitely many bounded-torsion rows. After inverting p we obtain

                         H ∗ (G, A)[1/p] ⊗ Qp [ϵ] ≃ H ∗ (GL3 (Zp ), Qp ) ⊗ Qp [ϵ].

The extended residue-field action is retained here. Witt Artin–Schreier gives the continuous
unramified calculation over W in [6, Lemma 3.8.5(1)]; its consequence for the full extended stabilizer
is [6, Lemma 3.8.5(2)]. Here P3 fixes W and the residue-field group acts by Witt Frobenius, exactly as
in the chosen Honda semilinear action. The coefficient conventions also agree: for every profinite Q,
                    a
cochains on Q × G with values in the discrete Wℓ (Fp ) have finite image. Lifting those finitely many
values makes cochain reduction surjective. Their derived inverse limit is therefore the continuous
W cochain complex. Every continuous cochain on this compact domain with values in W [1/p] has
bounded image, so inverting p gives the rational cochains used in part (2).
     To pass to the prescribed finite field k, put ∆k = Gal(Fp /k). Its action fixes u1 , u2 and acts by
coefficient Frobenius. Each quotient A/(p, u1 , u2 )a is a finite direct sum of truncated Witt coefficient
modules. Witt Artin–Schreier therefore identifies its ∆k -invariants with W (k)[[u1 , u2 ]]/(p, u1 , u2 )a
and makes its positive ∆k -cohomology vanish. The coefficient reductions, including cochains with
profinite parameters, are surjective by finite-value lifting. Taking the derived inverse limit gives

                                     RΓ(∆k , A) ≃ W (k)[[u1 , u2 ]],

equivariantly for P3 , since k contains the Honda endomorphism field. Hochschild–Serre now identifies
the algebraically closed residue-field calculation with the finite-field one. The trace-one contraction
of Lemma 3.2 supplies the finite semilinear descent used by Proposition 7.2.

Here J j is the image of the Moore ideal after removing invertible periodicity factors. The normalized

A. Their ideal is therefore m-primary. The pure-power ideals J j , whose exponents tend to infinity,
are cofinal with the powers of m, which proves the claimed equality of topologies from the restriction
kernels. Thus each composite βm : A → A is m-adically continuous. The coefficientwise Witt-vector
construction is therefore continuous. Its restriction f to W is the identity modulo p; continuity
and additivity make it Zp -linear. Write f = id + ph with a continuous Zp -linear endomorphism
h. The convergent series n≥0 (−ph)n is its inverse. Hence f −1 γ is an equivariant retraction and

A = W ⊕ A additively.
    The compact comparison used to compute the constants has the following integral hypotheses.
Choose a deep normal uniform open in P3 and in GL3 (Zp ). At p ≥ 5 its lower-p-series valuation
is saturated and equi-p-valued with denominator e = 1 and generators in degree one. The trivial
finite free module Zp satisfies the hypotheses of [27, Theorem 3.3.3]; its continuous comparison is
cup compatible. The coefficient and restriction naturality in [27, Theorem 3.1.1(1)–(3)] identifies
its rationalization with Lazard’s comparison [32, V.2.4.9–V.2.4.10] and makes it equivariant for
conjugation. Rational Hochschild–Serre then takes invariants under the finite quotient, whose
higher cohomology vanishes by averaging. The full exterior calculation for the compact matrix
and stabilizer groups, together with the Lie-algebra calculation and trivial adjoint action, is given
by [6, Proposition 3.8.1 and Lemma 3.8.2]. At height three its generator degrees are 1, 3, 5. The
unramified and finite semilinear descent from Step 4, with [6, Lemma 3.8.5(2)] for the full extended
stabilizer, therefore identifies both

                              H ∗ (G, W )[1/p]   and H ∗ (GL3 (Zp ), Qp )

with that exterior algebra over Qp .
    The displayed tower comparison makes the cohomology of A finite-dimensional in every degree.
                                                    c
Its additive decomposition into constants and A is a decomposition of continuous coefficient
                                  c
complexes. If di = dimQp H i (G, A )[1/p], comparison with the identical constant algebra, including
the common factor Qp [ϵ], gives di + di−1 = 0 (with d−1 = 0). Thus every di is zero. The constants
inclusion is a ring map, so its resulting cohomology isomorphism is multiplicative. This proves
(A.1).

                                                  108
   Apply the local relative Morava descent of Proposition 7.2 to T . The central procyclic subgroup
generated by 1 + p acts on internal degree 2t through (1 + p)−t . Its continuous two-term resolution
has differential (1 + p)−t − 1 on that coefficient module. For t ̸= 0 this scalar is nonzero in Qp , so
the rationalized two-term complex is acyclic; Hochschild–Serre makes every nonzero internal row
vanish. The locally proved relative spectral sequence is strongly convergent with finite cohomological
amplitude. Exact rationalization preserves its bounded convergence. Only the internal-degree-zero
row remains, with one filtration degree in each total degree. Multiplicativity therefore yields

                              π∗ L0 T = ΛQp (α1 , α3 , α5 ),   |αi | = −i.

This establishes the rational boundary used in Theorem 10.10; the image of its chosen primitive is
determined by the separate map calculation in Section 10.
